30.5. Moment map. The complex torus {(a1, · · · , an) ∈Cn| Q
i ai = 1} ∼= (C×)n−1 acts on
X with a the moment map (for the action of (S1)n−1) µ : X →a∗.
Each fixed point xλ =
(T λ1, · · · , T λn) corresponds to a cocharacter λ = (λ1, · · · , λn) ∈Zn (with P
i λi = 0). The torus
action preserves both stratifications and the image of each stratum closure is a convex polyhedron.
We can put all this information on the same diagram. Fix λ ∈χ∗(T)+. Let Vλ be the irreducible
representation of PGL3 with highest weight λ. Let Xλ be the K-orbit of the point xλ. Then µ(Xλ)
is the convex polyhedron spanned by the W orbit of the point λ. The lattice points µ inside this
polyhedron correspond to the weight spaces Vλ(µ). At each of these points the Kazhdan Lusztig
polynomial Pµ,λ(t) gives the dimension of this weight space.
For SL3 the moment map image gives the triangular lattice which can be interpreted as the
weight lattice for PGL3. The moment map image of the first stratum is a hexagon and in fact the
first stratum has the structure of a vector bundle over the flag manifold F1,2(C3). The closure of
this stratum consists of adding a single point, so it is the Thom space of this bundle. The local
intersection cohomology at the vertex (which appears at the origin in the diagram) is the primitive
cohomology of the flag manifold (that is, 1 + t).
Figure 29 represents the moment map image of the affine Grassmannian for SL3; it also equals
the weight diagram for PGL3(C). The dotted red lines are reflecting hyperplanes for the Weyl
group. The red dot is a highest weight λ for PGLe; the other dots are their Weyl images. The
blue hexagon is the outline of the moment map images; it is also the collection of weights in the
irreducible representation of highest weight λ. The 1 and 1 + t beneth the dots are the Kazhdan
Lusztig polynomials.
30.6. Perverse sheaves on X. Throughout this section for simplicity let G = SLn(C((T))),
K = SLn(C[[T]]) and X = G/K. We would like to imitate the construction with the flag manifold,
and create a convolution product for sheaves on X × X that are constructible with respect to the
stratification by G orbits, that is, if pij : X × X × X →X × X as before, set
A• ◦B• = Rp13∗(p∗
12(A•) ⊗p∗
23(B•)).
Unfortunately the orbits of G on X × X have infinite dimension and infinite codimension so this
simply does not make sense. V. Ginzburg and (later) K. Vilonen and I. Mirkoviˇc found a way
around this problem.
Let P(X) denote the category of perverse sheaves, constructible with respect to the above orbit
stratification of X, cf. §21.13. (Ginzburg shows this is equivalent to the category of K-equivariant
perverse sheaves on X.) Since each stratum is simply connected the local systems associated to
these sheaves are trivial. It turns out that the intersection cohomology sheaves live only in even
degrees and this implies that every perverse sheaf is isomorphic to a direct sum of IC• sheaves of
stratum closures.
Mirkoviˇc and Vilonen define a tensor product structure on P(X) as follows. Consider the diagram

30.9. Remarks. Theorem 30.7 should be regarded as a categorification of Satake’s isomorphism.
In fact, taking the K group of the Grothendieck group on both sides gives
K(P(X)) ∼= H(G, K) ∼= χ∗(T)W
which is the classical Satake isomorphism.
If we could duplicate the construction in the finite dimensional case we would consider G orbits
on X × X. Ginzburg, Mirkoviˇc and Vilonen replace X × X with G ×K X which is a fiber bundle
over X with fiber isomorphic to X. They replace the G orbits with the strata Sλ,µ which is a fiber
bundle over Xµ with fiber isomorphic to Xλ.
It is totally nonobvious that the convolution of perverse sheaves is perverse. This depends on
the fact that the mapping m : G ×K X →X is semi-small in a very strong sense. For each
λ, µ ∈χ∗(T)+ let Sλ,µ = p−1(Xλ) ×K Xµ.
These form a stratification of G ×K X.
It turns


PAGE 124
124
MARK GORESKY
out that the restriction m : Sλ,µ →X is semi-small (onto its image, which is a union of strata
Xτ). This implies that Rm∗(E[dλ,µ]) is perverse, for any locally constant sheaf E on Sλ,µ (where
dλ,µ = dim(Sλ,µ).
30.10. Tannakian category. (see [38]) There is a general theorem that a reductive algebraic
group (say, over an algebraically closed field) can be recovered from its cartegory of representations.
More generally if C is a tensor category, that is, an abelian category together with a “tensor”
structure (A, B ∈C
=⇒
A ◦B ∈C) that is commutative and associative in a functorial way
(satisfies the associativity constraint and the commutativity constraint, and if h : C →{V S} is a
rigid fiber functor (that is, an exact functor to vector spaces such that h(A ◦B) = h(A) ⊗h(B))
then the group of automorphisms of h (that is, the group of natural transformations h →h) is an
algebraic group G whose category of representations is equivalent to the original category C.
It turns out that P(X) is such a Tannakian category and that h is a rigid fiber functor. Therefore
the group of automorphisms of h is isomorphic to the Langlands dual group LG.
31. Vanishing cycles and all that
31.1. This section is parallel to §17.3 but the objects in this section are globally defined. The
proofs in this section are outlined with emphasis on the geometric ideas but detailed rigorous
proofs of the theorems in this section may be found in [1] §4 and Appendix B.5.
Throughout this section we fix a complex algebraic (resp. analytic) variety X and a proper
algebraic (resp. analytic) regular mapping f : X →C, to be thought of as a family of varieties,
Xt = f −1(t) as t ∈C varies in a neighborhood of 0. Throughout this section we use the “classical”
indexing scheme for cohomology degrees. (Note that [1] uses Deligne’s numbering conventions so
the formulas there involve different shifts.)
31.2. Monodromy. Choose algebraic (resp. analytic) Whitney stratifications of X and C so that
f is a stratified mapping that takes strata submersively to strata. The stratification of C consists
of a finite (resp. discrete) set of points Z ⊂C which we assume contains 0 ∈C. It follows from
the isotopy lemma that f is a fiber bundle over C \ Z, while the “special fiber” X0 = f −1(0) is a
closed union of strata of X. Each “generic fiber” Xt is stratified by its intersection with the strata
of X.
Let ∆= Dδ(0) be the closed disk of radius δ taken sufficiently small that ∆∩Z = {0} and
let ∂∆∼= S1 denote its boundary. Fix t ∈∂∆. Since f −1(∂∆) →∂∆is a fiber bundle there is a
monodromy homeomorphism
µ : Xt →Xt
where Xt = f −1(t). In fact, f −1(∂∆) is canonically stratified by its intersection with the strata of
X so the vector field ∂/∂θ on ∂∆has a controlled lift whose flow preserves strata. Taking µ to be
the time 1 flow of this lift guarantees that it preserves strata and is smooth on each stratum.


PAGE 125
LECTURE NOTES ON SHEAVES AND PERVERSE SHEAVES
125
31.3. Nearby cycles. As in §8.11 there is a neighborhood N of X0 and a canonical retraction
r : N →X0 (defined up to stratum preserving homotopy, see Figure 5). Choose δ sufficiently
small that ∆∩Z = {0} and f −1(∆) ⊂N. Choose t ∈∂∆and consider the restriction
Ψ = r|Xt : Xt →X0.
If A• is a (constructible) complex of sheaves on X \ X0 (or just a complex of sheaves on X) then
the sheaf
(31.3.1)
Ψ∗(A•) := RΨ∗(A•|Xt)
is called the topological sheaf of nearby cycles on X0. It follows immediately that the sheaf Ψ∗(A•)
gives a way to calculate the cohomology of Xt in terms of data on X0, that is,
H∗(X0; Ψ∗(A•)) ∼= H∗(Xt; A•).
The retraction r depends on a choice of stratification of X so that X0 is a union of strata. It
also depends on a choice of control data for this stratification, in particular, a system of tubular
neighborhoods πS : TS →S of each stratum S. For each stratum S of X0 the retraction r collapses
the fibers of πS.
Suppose S = {x0} is a zero dimensional stratum. Then TS = Bϵ(x0) is an open ball centered
at x0. As in §17.3 if ϵ is sufficiently small then ∂Bϵ(x0) will be transverse to each stratum of X.
This defines a stratification of the closed ball Bϵ(x0). Fixing ϵ, if δ > 0 is sufficiently small and
t ∈∆= Dδ(0) ⊂C then each stratum of Xt will be transverse to each stratum of Bϵ(x0) and
∂Bϵ(0), a situation that is (sloppily) denoted 0 < δ << ϵ. The intersection
Mt(f) = Xt ∩Bϵ(0)
is called the Milnor fiber associated to the mapping f at the point x0. Its stratified isomorphism
type and complex analytic structure are independent of the choice of δ << ϵ ([59] §6.13).
The monodromy restricts to a stratified mapping µ : Mt(f) →Mt(f). In the case that f is
a generic linear projection Cn →C the Milnor fiber coincides with the complex link L of the
stratum S in X, cf. §17.3 and the restriction µ|∂Mt(f) is the identity but in the general case the
monodromy acts nontrivially on the boundary of the Milnor fiber, cf. [108].
The retraction r : N →X0 collapses the neighborhood Bϵ(x0) to the point {x0}. Consequently
Ψ−1(x0) = Xt ∩Bϵ(x0) = Mt(f)
so the stalk cohomology of Ψ∗(A•) at x0 is the the cohomology H∗(Mt(f); A•) of the Milnor fiber.
If the point x0 lies in some stratum S of Xt the stalk cohomology of RΨ∗(A•) is the cohomology
of the Milnor fiber of the normal slice (§8.3) to S at the point x0. (There is a tricky point if R < S
are strata and x0 ∈S ∩TR(2ϵ), as in Figure 5. In fact the fiber r−1(x0) of the retraction is the
normal slice to the stratum S at some other point x1 ∈S because the retraction r also shrinks
points in S ∩TR(2ϵ) towards the stratum R. The isotopy lemmas guarantee that these normal
slices are isomorphic.) It follows that the sheaf of nearby cycles RΨ∗(A•|Xt) is (cohomologically)
constructible with respect to the stratification of X0.

MARK GORESKY

31.11. Proposition. Specialization commutes with Verdier duality. Both Ψf and Φf take
perverse sheaves to perverse sheaves.
Proof. Using the notation of §31.3, the value t ∈C is a regular value of f : X →C, meaning that
for each stratum S of X, if y ∈S ∩F −1(t) then the differential df(y) : TyS →C is surjective).
Therefore Xt is stratified by its intersection with the strata of X in a manner that preserves
codimension, which is to say that Xt →X is normally nonsingular . Assume that A• is a perverse
sheaf. It follows that the restriction A•|t is perverse on Xt. (In Deligne’s numbering A•|Xt[−1] is
perverse.)
The specialization map ψ : Xt →X0 is proper so ψ∗commutes with Verdier duality by Theorem
12.3 part (4), and it suffices to verify that Ψ∗(A•|Xt) satisfies the support conditions of a perverse
sheaf on X0. The stalk cohomology of Ψ∗(A•|Xt) is the cohomology of the Milnor fiber Mt. The
Morse theoretic argument in the proof of Theorem 17.7 (which concerns the complex link) applies
verbatim to the Milnor fiber . By Theorem 17.7 this gives
Hr(RΨ∗(A•|Xt))x0 ∼= Hr(Mt; A•|Mt) = 0 for all r > dimC(Mt) = dim(X0)
which says that RΨ∗(A•)[1] satisfies the support conditions of a perverse sheaf.


PAGE 130
130
MARK GORESKY
To prove that Φf(A•) is perverse, it again suffices to check that it satisfies the support conditions
of a perverse sheaf at each point and it suffices to check this at the point x0. Consider the long exact
sequence on stalk cohomology corresponding to the triangle (31.9.1). Since Ψf(A•) is perverse its
stalk cohomology vanishes in degrees r > dim(X0). Since A• is perverse on X its stalk cohomology
vanishes in degrees r > dim(X) = dim(X0) + 1. It follows that the stalk cohomology of Φf(A•)
vanishes in degrees r > dim(X0).
□
31.12. Using stratified Morse theory in 1978, MacPherson and I were surprised to discover that
the middle IC sheaf does not specialize to the middle IC• sheaf, but rather it specializes to
the logarithmic IC• sheaf and even more surprisingly, the logarithmic IC• sheaf specializes to
the logarithmic IC• sheaf.
We double and triple checked these calculations.
When perverse
sheaves were discovered in 1981 we realized that this calculation actually proved that perverse
sheaves specialize to perverse sheaves. This Morse theoretic argument was presented at the Luminy
conference in July 1981, see [120] for the proceedings17. At that time O. Gabber gave a proof in
´etale cohomology which is now in [16], cf. Verdier’s article [136].
32. Cellular perverse sheaves
32.1. The theory of cellular perverse sheaves was developed by R. MacPherson and described in
lectures [99, 100] and was explained in detail in the thesis [140, 139] of M. Vybornov. Cellular
perverse sheaves interpolate between cellular sheaves and cellular cosheaves in a remarkable way.
The theory was extended and simplified in [112, 138, 141].
Suppose K is a finite simplicial complex18. We will be sloppy and identify K with its geometric
realization |K|. Each simplex σ is a closed subset of K and we denote its interior by σo which
we refer to as a cell. If σ ⊆τ we write σ ≤τ and similarly for proper inclusions. We also write
σ ↔τ if either σ ≤τ or τ ≤σ. The barycenter of a simplex σ is denoted ˆσ. An r-simplex θ in
the barycentric subdivision K′ is the span θ = ⟨ˆσ0, ˆσ1, · · · , ˆσr⟩where σ0 < σ1 < · · · < σr. If S is
a collection of vertices of K then its span consists of those simplices σ such that every vertex of σ
is in S.
32.2. Simplicial derived category. (See also §2.1, §2.5.) We summarize some results in the
thesis of Allen Shepard [125] that were developed in a seminar at Brown University in 1977-78.
We consider sheaves of Q-vector spaces but the coefficients may be taken in any field.
Let Db
c(K) denote the bounded derived category of sheaves on K that are cohomologically
constructible with respect to the stratification by cells (i.e. interiors of simplices).
17The 1981 Luminy conference proceedings in Ast´erisque 101-102 appeared before Ast´erisque 100. The Editors
of Ast´erisque reserved the number 100 for the book Faisceaux Pervers but the manuscript was not available until
after Ast´erisque 101-102 was published.
18Statements in this section hold without modification for any finite strongly regular cell complex, meaning that
the closure of each cell is homeomorphic to a closed ball, and the intersection of two (closed) cells is a cell, or else
empty. Beware that the Schubert cell decomposition of the flag and Grassmann manifold is not regular.

Recall §2.1 that a simplicial sheaf A assigns to each simplex σ a finite dimensional Q-vector
space Aσ and to each face σ < τ a restriction homomorphism sστ : Aσ →Aτ in a way that is
compatible with compositions. Morphisms of simplicial sheaves are required to be compatible with
these restriction mappings. The category of simplicial sheaves is equivalent to the subcategory of
Db
c(K) whose objects have stalk cohomology only in degree 0 (that is, the category of ¯0-perverse
sheaves).
If A, B are simplicial sheaves, the sheaf Hom(A, B) is the simplicial sheaf
Hom(A, B)(σ) = Hom(A|St(σ), B|St(σ))
where St(σ) denotes the open star of σ.
So the group of sections Hom(A, B)(σ) consists of homomorphisms A(σ) →B(σ) that are
compatible with all corestriction maps emanating from σ.
Let C be a cell (or stratum) of K. Let i : C →K be the inclusion. The closure of its image
is a simplex σ and Ri∗(QC) = i∗(QC) = Qσ is the constant sheaf on the (closed) simplex σ. To
streamline notation we denote this by Qσ∗and set Qσ! = i!(QC), the extension by zero. A key
exercise is to verify the following:

direct sum of such elementary injectives.

32.4. Simplicial cosheaves. There are two ways to view a simplicial cosheaf R on a simplicial
complex K. The first, as a pre-cosheaf that assigns to each simplex σ a vector space Rσ and to
each incidence σ < τ a homomorphism Aτ →Aσ,in a way that is compatible with compositions.
The second way involves the dual cells of K.
Let K′ denote the first barycentric subdivision of K. For each simplex σ in K the dual cell D(σ)
is the union of those simplices x in K′ spanned by ˆσ and the barycenters ˆτ of simplices τ > σ.
(Although it is contractible to ˆσ the dual cell D(σ) is not necessarily homeomorphic to a disk.) If
σ < τ then D(τ) ⊂D(σ) and we write D(τ) < D(σ).
τ1
τ2
τ3
σ
Dual of σ in blue


PAGE 134
134
MARK GORESKY
A sheaf B on the dual cell decomposition assigns a vector space Bξ to each dual cell ξ = D(σ)
and a homomorphism Bξ →Bη whenever ξ < η in a way that is compatible with compositions.
So a sheaf on the dual cell decomposition is the same thing as a cosheaf on the cell decomposition.
32.5. Perversities. We follow the notation of [16] by modfying the definition of a perversity p
to allow for codimension one strata and considering p to be a function of the dimension of the
stratum. This results in a shift of cohomological degree that depends on p. We refer to the notation
of §21.14.
Although it is possible to develop the theory of simplicial perverse sheaves for perversities that
vary with the simplex, in these notes we assume for simplicity that the perversity p(k) ≤0 is a
function only of the dimension k of the simplex19 with p(0) = 0 and p(k −1) ≥p(k) ≥p(k −1)
for all k.
Let C be a k-dimensional cell of K, with closure σ. Following [112] we say that C (or its closure
σ or its dimension k) is of type ! if p(k) = p(k −1) and is of type * if p(k) = p(k −1) −1.
The number k = 0 is considered to be both types. The simple objects in the category of perverse
sheaves are the complexes IC•
σ. From §32.3, and observed in [112], for any simplex σ:
The intersection complex extending the constant sheaf Qσo on the interior σo is:

if σ is type !
32.6. Perverse dimension. MacPherson’s insight ([99, 100]) was to define the perverse dimen-
sion of a d-simplex σ (or of C = σo) to be
δ(σ) =
(
−p(d) ≥0
if σ has type *
−p(d) −d ≤0
if σ has type !
Therefore |δ(σ)| is the number of integers k with 1 ≤k ≤d of the same type as d. Then δ : Z≥0 →Z
is one to one, and for each d the image δ([0, d]) is a subinterval of integers in [−d, d] containing 0.

it is p-perverse with respect to the stratification S if and only if it is p-perverse with respect to the triangulation.

32.7. Partial order. There is a unique partial order on the set of simplices of K that is generated
by the following elementary relation:
σ ⪰τ if σ ↔τ and δ(σ) = δ(τ) + 1, in other words, two
simplices σ, τ satisfy σ ⪰τ if there is a sequence of elementary relations σ = σ0 ⪰σ1 · · · ⪰σr = τ.
It is easy to check the following.
Suppose σ ⪰τ. If both σ, τ are type * then τ ⊂σ. If both are type ! then σ ⊂τ. If they are
opposite types then σ is type * (so δ(σ) > 0) and τ has type ! (so δ(τ) < 0) and σ ∩τ ̸= ϕ.
Hence an arrow σ ⪰τ can extend no farther than adjacent simplices. This may happen if
δ(σ) and δ(τ) have opposite signs, for example, dim(σ) = 1, dim(τ) = 2 and δ(0, 1, 2) = 0, 1, −1
respectively.

32.8. Perverse cells. For each simplex σ the corresponding perverse cell δσ is the following union
of simplices in the barycentric subdivision K′:
δσ =
[
{⟨ˆτ0ˆτ1 · · · ˆτr⟩| ∀j, δ(τj) ≤δ(σ) and ∃i with τi = σ}
In this equation τ0 < τ1 < · · · < τr form a partial flag of simplices hence δ(τj) takes distinct
values. The “boundary” ∂(δσ) (also called the perverse link of σ) is the union of those simplices
⟨ˆτ0ˆτ1 · · · ˆτr⟩in δσ such that δ(τj) < δ(σ) for all j (0 ≤j ≤r). (That is, remove the vertex ˆσ from
each simplex.) If σ is type ! then δσ ⊂D(σ). If σ is type * then σ ⊂δσ and ∂σ ⊂∂δσ. The
interior is δσo = δσ −∂δσ. If C = τ o is the interior of τ we sometimes denote δσo by δC. The key
technical point in the theory is the following, which implies the functor Hr
δC from the category of
perverse sheaves to vector spaces is exact.

Remark. Rather than the “chain complex” relations (32.11.1) one might consider objects T which
assign to each simplex σ a vector space Tσ and attaching homomorphisms Tσ →Tτ whenever σ ↔τ
with δ(σ) = δ(τ) + 1, that are compatible with compositions. The resulting category turns out to
be the Koszul dual category, see §33.5.
32.13. Verdier duality. It is easy to check that Verdier duality in the category of sheaves on
K constructible with respect to the triangulation exchanges the category of perverse sheaves with
perversity p and associated function δ with the category of perverse sheaves with perversity q = t−p
and associated function −δ (where t(d) = −d is the “top” perversity). It reverses the arrows of
the quiver Q associated to δ (§33.2).
32.14. Remarks.
1. If p = 0 is the zero perversity then every cell is type ! and δσ = D(σ) is the dual of the simplex
σ. The perverse link ∂δσ is the “usual” link of σ. A perverse sheaf A is a simplicial sheaf in the
usual sense, except that signs must be added (which is always possible in this case) to the mappings
sστ so as to obtain sθτsσθ = sστ. The complex (32.11.1) computes the cellular cohomology of A.
cf. §2.1.
If p is the top perversity then δσ = σ and a perverse sheaf A is a simplicial cosheaf (§32.4) in the
usual sense, up to the same issue involving signs. The complex (32.11.1) computes its homology.
2. One might attempt to mimic Proposition 32.12 using the filtration by closed subsets,
· · · ⊂Kr−1 ⊂Kr ⊂Kr+1 ⊂· · ·
in order to obtain a chain complex with terms
Ht(Kr, Kr−1; A) ∼=
M
δ(σ)=r
Ht(δσ, ∂δσ, A).
Unfortunately the relative cohomology H∗(δσ, ∂δσ; A) may live in several degrees as the perverse
sheaf A varies. Let K = τ be the 3-simplex, let p(3) = −1 and p(d) = 0 otherwise, so that
δ(3) = 1 and δ(d) = −d for d = 0, 1, 2. Let σ be a vertex of τ. Then H0(δσ, ∂δσ; ICσ) = Q but
H1(δσ, ∂δσ; ICτ) = Q.
3. In the example of §32.7 the sum (32.11.1) contains a single term, so that sστ = 0. In general,
if σ, τ are simplices of K such that δ(σ) and δ(τ) have opposite signs, and σ ⪰τ but σ ̸↔τ then
any composition
s(σr, τ) ◦s(σr−1, σr) ◦· · · ◦s(σ1, σ2) ◦s(σ, σ1)... = 0
of attaching homomorphisms from Aσ to Aτ will vanish because such a composition eventually
passes through a triple θ1 ⪰θ0 ⪰θ−1 with δ = +1, 0, −1 respectively and θ1 ̸↔θ−1. Then θ0 is a
vertex and one of the other two is a 1-simplex which meets the remaining simplex in a single point,
{θ0}. The chain complex requirement for this triple has only one term: s(θ0, θ−1)s(θ1, θ0) = 0.


PAGE 140
140
MARK GORESKY
4. In the more general situation when K is a regular cell complex the preceding argument fails.
In this case the definition of a cellular perverse sheaf requires the additional locality axiom ([99]):
If σ ⪰τ and σ ̸↔τ then any composition of morphisms from Aσ to Aτ vanishes.

33. The path algebra
33.1. Aside from standard definitions the material in this section is due to Vybornov [138, 140,
141, 142] and Polishchuk [112]. Throughout this section we retain the notation of §32: K is a
finite simplicial complex, p is a perversity with associated function δ, For simplices σ, τ in K we
write σ ↔τ if either σ < τ or τ < σ. We denote by C the category of cellular perverse sheave of
Q-vector spaces on K.
33.2. Associated to K, δ there is a quiver Q, or directed graph without oriented cycles, whose
vertices are the simplices of K and whose arrows correspond to elementary relations σ ≻τ with
δ(σ) = δ(τ) + 1. There is a graded path algebra F = L
j≥0 Fj, which is generated by elements
of degree ≤1. The vector space F0 has a basis element [σ] for each simplex σ in K (the “trivial
paths’), and F1 has a (canonical) basis consisting of the elementary relations, that is, [sστ] where
σ ↔τ and δ(σ) = δ(τ) + 1, subject to the following relations
(1) [σ]2 = [σ]


PAGE 141
LECTURE NOTES ON SHEAVES AND PERVERSE SHEAVES
141
(2) [σ].[τ] = 0 unless τ = σ.
(3) [τ].[sστ] = [sστ].[σ] = [sστ]
These relations already imply that [sστ].[sαβ] = 0 unless σ = β.
If σ ⪰τ and δ(σ) = δ(τ) + r (with r ≥1) then there is a sequence of elementary relations
σ = σ0 ⪰σ1 ⪰· · · ⪰σr = τ connecting them. It follows that the vector space Fr has a (canonical)
basis whose elements are paths of length r with decreasing δ.
Products in F corresponds to
concatenation of paths. Consequently a representation of the quiver Q (that is, a vector space Aσ
for each simplex and a homomorphism Aσ →Aτ for each elementary relation σ ≻τ) is the same
thing as a module over the path algebra F. Given a module M the associated vector spaces are
given by Mσ = [σ].M.
Not every representation of Q corresponds to a perverse sheaf: the relations (32.11.2) are required
to hold. From these relations we immediately conclude
33.3. Proposition. The category P(K) of perverse sheaves, constructible with respect to the
triangulation of K is naturally equivalent to the category Mod −B of modules over the ring
B = F/J where J ⊂F is the two-sided homogeneous ideal generated by the vector subspace
E ⊂F2 spanned by the elements uστ whenever δ(σ) = r + 1 and δ(τ) = r −1 defined by

33.4. The Ext algebra. The ring B is a quadratic algebra. The Koszul dual of such an algebra
is sometimes defined to be the Ext algebra of the simple B-modules. Following [112] we consider

33.7. Proposition. [139] The ring B and the Ext algebra A are quadratic duals.
Proof. In fact the subspaces D, E ⊂F2 above are orthogonal complements. The vector space F2
in the path algebra is the orthogonal direct sum of the subspaces Vστ spanned by paths of length
two, that is,
(33.7.1)
Vστ = span {[sxτ].[sσx]| σ ⪰x ⪰τ, δ(σ) = δ(x) + 1 = δ(τ) + 2} .
We claim that within each Vστ the corresponding subspaces Dστ, Eστ ⊂F2 are orthogonal comple-
ments. Fix σ, τ as in (33.7.1) and first suppose they are both of type *. This implies that σ ↔τ
and so, given σ, τ there are two or more choices for x. The resulting vectors (33.5.1) span the
subspace Dστ that is orthogonal to the vector uστ of (33.3.1) which spans Eστ. The same holds if
σ, τ are both of type !. If σ, τ are different types then δ(σ) = 1, δ(τ) = −1, δ(x) = 0 and either
σ or τ is one-dimensional, cf. the example in §32.7. Then {x} = σ ∩τ is the unique point in the
intersection so the sum (33.3.1) has a single term: it is the monomial [sxτ].[sσx]. Hence Vστ = Eστ
is one dimensional, while Dστ = 0.
□.

Appendix A. Essential21 Category Theory
A.1. Definitions. A category C has a class of objects and, for any pair (X, Y ) of objects, a set
HomC(X, Y ) of morphisms, elements of which are denoted by arrows X →Y . (It is common to
abuse notation and write X ∈C when X is an object of C.) There is a unique identity morphism
idX in HomC(X, X). Composition of morphisms is defined with
X
f
−→Y
g
−→Z denoted g ◦f : X →Z.
Composition is associative: h(gf) = (hg)f and idX ◦f = f = f ◦idY whenever f : X →Y .
A morphism f : X →Y is monic if: for any object Z and any two distinct h1 ̸= h2 ∈Hom(Z, X)
the compositions fhx ̸= fhx are not equal. The morphism f : X →Y is epi if: for any object Z
and any two distinct h1 ̸= h2 ∈Hom(Y, Z) the compositions h1f ̸= h2f are not equal.
If C, D are categories, a (covariant) functor F : C →D is a rule that assigns to each object
X ∈C another object F(X) ∈D and for any morphism f : X →Y in C another morphism
F(f) : F(X) →F(Y ) in D, in a way that is compatible with composition and which takes
identities to identites. The category C is a full subcategory if the functor F induces an isomorphism
HomC(x, y) ∼= HomD(F(x), F(y)).
A.2. A comment about categories. The Zermelo Fraenkel axioms avoid Russell’s paradox
by providing classes of objects that are “too big” to be sets. In a category C the collection of
morphisms HomC(X, Y ) between two objects X, Y is assumed to form a set, and so we may speak
of two morphisms f, g being the same, and we write f = g. However the collection of objects may
fail to form a set and so the statement that “Y = X” or “Y is the same object as X” does not
make sense. Rather, “the morphism f : X →Y is an isomorphism” is the correct way to indicate
an identification between two objects in a category. If the set HomC(X, X) of self-isomorphisms
of X is nontrivial, there will be many distinct isomorphisms between X and Y .
21Saunders MacLane recalled that the first paper [41] on category theory was initially rejected by the editor of a
prestigious journal, who stated that he had never before seen a paper that was so entirely devoid of content. Today,
many results cannot even be stated without an appropriate category, cf. §11.3.


PAGE 144
144
MARK GORESKY
For example, all the objects in the category of 1-dimensional real vector spaces are isomorphic,
however an isomorphism h : R →V corresponds to a choice of a nonzero element x ∈V by setting
h(1) = x.
A.3. Abelian categories. An Abelian category C is one in which every HomC(X, Y ) has the
structure of an Abelian group such that composition of morphisms is distributive over addition
(g(f + f ′)h = gfh + gf ′h) and furthermore
(1) There exists a zero object 0 so that HomC(0, 0) is the Abelian group {0}
(2) Any two objects X, Y have a direct sum object X ⊕Y with morphisms
X
p1
←−
−→
p2 X ⊕Y
(3) Every f : X →Y has a kernel, an image and a cokernel in C with morphisms
ker(f)
- X
f
- Y
- coker(f)
Im(f)
-
-
(4) If f : X →Y then the natural map
coker (ker(f) →X) →ker (Y →coker(f))
is an isomorphism. (So it defines Im(f).)
A.4. Definition. An indecomposable object A in an abelian category is one that cannot
be expressed nontrivially as a direct sum A = B ⊕C. A simple object A is one that has
no nontrivial subobjects B →A (where the morphism is a monomorphism). An object
is semisimple if it is a direct sum of simple objects. An object is Artinian if descending
chains stabilize. It is Noetherian if ascending chains stabilize. A category is Artinian (resp.
Noetherian) if every object is.
Each object in an Artinian Noetherian category can be
expressed as a finite iterated sequence of extensions of simple objects.
A.5. Adjoint functors. If C, D are categories, f : C →D and g : D →C are functors, then f
and g are said to be adjoint if there are “natural” isomorphisms
ΦAB : HomC(A, g(B)) →HomD(f(A), B)
for all A, B objects of C, D respectively (where “natural” means that these isomorphism are com-
patible with all morphisms A1 →A2 and B1 →B2). We say that f is left adjoint to g and g is
right adjoint to f.
In this case, f essentially determines g and vice versa, that is, given f and two adjoint functors
g1, g2 then there exists a natural equivalence of functors between g1, g2. On a formal level this is
a consequence of the Yoneda Lemma, but it can be seen directly. We have a natural isomorphism


PAGE 145
LECTURE NOTES ON SHEAVES AND PERVERSE SHEAVES
145
HomC(A, g1(B)) ∼= HomC(A, g2(B)) for every A ∈C, B ∈D. Taking A = g1(B), the identity on
the left side gives an element τ(B) ∈HomC(g1(B), g2(B)). It is an exercise to see that this defines
a natural transformation g1
=⇒
g2, in other words, if h : B →B′ is a morphism in D then
g2(h) ◦τ(B) = τ(B′) ◦g1(h). Since each ΦAB is an isomorphism, it follows that τ is a natural
equivalence.
Examples. If H is a subgroup of a finite group G then “restriction” is a more or less obvious functor
from the category of representations of G to the category of representations of H; its adjoint turns
out to be “induction”, a much more subtle construction, and the adjointness property is classically
known as the Frobenius reciprocity theorem,
HomG(W, IndG
H(V )) ∼= HomH(W|H, V ).
Sheafification (§1.4) is adjoint to the inclusion of categories (sheaves →presheaves). If f : X →Y
is a continuous mapping then Proposition 1.12 says that f ∗is left adjoint to f∗. One might ask
whether there is an adjoint to the functor f!. This turns out to be a very subtle question, see
§11.5.
A.6. Exact functors and injective objects. A functor F : C →D between Abelian categories
C, D is exact if it takes each short exact sequence 0 →A →B →C →0 to a short exact sequence
0 →F(A) →F(B) →F(C) →0. It is left exact 0 →F(A) →F(B) →F(C) is exact. It is right
exact if F(A) →F(B) →F(C) →0 is exact. For any object B in C the (contravariant) functor
A 7→HomC(A, B) is left exact.
An object I in C is injective if A 7→HomC(A, I) is an exact functor. Equivalently, Suppose
f : A →B is an injective morphism in C. Then any morphism h : A →I extends to a morphism
˜h : B →I.
An object P is projective if B 7→HomC(P, B) is exact. Equivalently, suppose g : B →A is a
surjective morphism in C. Then any morphism k : P →A factors through some ˜k : P →B.

A.14. Homotopy theory. Two morphisms f, g : A• →B• of complexes are said to be homotopic
if there is a collection of mappings h : Ar →Br−1 so that hdA+dBh = f −g. This is an equivalence
relation. Equivalence classes are referred to as homotopy classes of maps; the set of which is denoted
[A•, B•].
A cool fact is that homotopy classes of chain maps are exactly the cohomology of the Hom•
complex:
A.15. Lemma. (exercise) If A•, B• are complexes in an Abelian category then (by changing
the sign of half the differentials) a chain map f : A• →B• determines a cycle
dHomf = 0 ∈Hom•(A•, B•)
and consequently Hn Hom•(A•, B•) ∼= [A•, B•[n]].

A.17. Lemma. Let f : A• →B• be a quasi-isomorphism of (bounded below) complexes.

A.18. Injective resolution. A category C has enough injectives if every object A embeds in
some injective object I. In this case, there is an exact sequence, classically called an injective
resolution,
0 →A →I0 →I1 →· · ·
where Ir are injective. It is easily constructed by first embedding A in an injective object I0 then
embedding K = coker(A →I0) in an injective object I1 and continuing by induction.
Example. The integers has the injective resolution 0 →Z →Q →Q/Z →0 and more generally
if R is an integral domain with fraction field F then 0 →R →F →F/R →0 is an injective
resolution of R. The ring R = Z/(4) is injective over itself and if we consider Z/(2) to be an R
module then it has a resolution 0 →Z/(2)
·2
−→Z/(4)
·2
−→Z/(4)
·2
−→· · · .
Verdier extended this notion of injective resolution to complexes:
A.19. Definition. An injective resolution of a complex A• is a quasi-isomorphism A• →I•
where each Ir is injective.
This fits beautifully with the classical definition, if we consider a single object A to be a complex
0 −−−→A −−−→0 −−−→0 −−−→. . .

A.20. Proposition. Suppose the Abelian category C has enough injectives.
(1) every complex A• has an injective resolution A• →I•.
(2) Let A• →I• and B• →J• be injective resolutions of complexes A•, B•. Then any
morphism f : A• →B• admits a lift ˜f : I• →J• and any two such lifts are homtopic.
(3) Any two injective resolutions of A• are homotopy equivalent.

A.22. Derived category: Preliminary model. Let C be an Abelian category. The derived
category D(C) is a category of complexes in which quasi-isomorphisms have become isomorphisms.
There is no simple way to arrange this.
For a preliminary model, let us say the bounded derived category Ib(C) is the category whose
objects are complexes A• of injective objects in C whose cohomologyis bounded (meaning that
Hr(A•) = 0 for sufficiently large r and for sufficiently small r). The morphisms are homotopy
classes of morphisms (of complexes), so that Ib(C) is the homotopy category of (complexes of)
injective objects.
Let Kb(C) be the homotopy category of complexes A• of objects in C with bounded cohomology:
Choosing an injective resolution for each complex gives a functor from Kb(D) →Ib(C). According
to Proposition ?? and Lemma A.17 it takes quasi-isomorphisms to isomorphisms. .
A.23. The derived category: second construction. The preliminary model has the disad-
vantage that it is adapted only for right derived functors, and besides, injective objects are usually
intractable. A more balanced point of view constructs the derived category as a “quotient cat-
egory” of the homotopy category Kb(C) of complexes, by inverting quasi-isomorphisms.
This
gives a way of referring to elements of the derived category without having to injectively resolve.
Moreover, each complex of sheaves is automatically an object in the derived category Db(C).
Let Db(C) be the category whose objects are complexes of elements of C and where a morphism
A• →B• is an equivalence class of “roof” diagrams

A.24. Proposition. The natural functor Ib(X) →Db(X) is an equivalence of categories.
Proof. To show that a functor F : C →D is an equivalence of categories it suffices to show (a)
that it is essentially surjective, meaning that every object in D is isomorphic to an object F(C) for
some object C in C, and (b) that F induces an isomorphism on Hom sets. The first part (a) is clear
because we have injective resolutions. Part (b) follows immediately from the fact (Lemma A.17)
that a quasi-isomorphism of injective complexes is a homotopy equivalence and has a homotopy
inverse.
□
A.25. Derived functors. Let F : C →D be a left exact functor between Abelian categories and
assume that C has enough injectives. Its derived functor RF : Db(C) →Db(D) is defined to be
RF(A•) = F(I•) where A• →I• is an23 The value RF(ϕ) is defined on morphisms ϕ : A• →B•
as above, because (Proposition A.20) ϕ extends to a morphism I• →J• on the corresponding
injective resolutions of A•, B•.
If F is right exact and if C has enough projectives then the left derived functor LF : Db(C →D)
is similarly defined to be LF(A•) = F(P •) where P • →A• is a projective resolution.
Remark. Although T(I•) is an object in Db(D) the individual terms T(Ir) are not necessarily
injective unless T also takes injectives to injectives.
If V : D →E is another functor then
R(V ◦T)(A•) = (RV )(T(I•)) = V (J•) where T(I•) →J• is an injective resolution of T(I•).
A.26. T-acyclic resolutions. Consider a functor T : C →D between abelian categories. An
object X ∈C is T-acyclic if RiT(X) = 0 for all i ̸= 0.
If T is exact then (exercise) every object X is T-acyclic. The great advantage of T-acyclic
objects is that they may be often used in place of injective objects when computing the derived
functors of T.
A.27. Lemma. Let T : C →D be a left exact functor between Abelian categories with enough
injectives. Let A• be a complex of objects in C and let A• →X• be a quasi-isomorphism,
where each of the objects Xr is T-acyclic. Then RrT(A•) is canonically isomorphic to the
r-th cohomology object of the complex
T(X0) →T(X1) →T(X2) →· · · .
If T is exact then there is no need to take a resolution at all: RT(A•) ∼= T(A•).

category of sheaves this can be done canonically using “the” Godement resolutioninjective resolution.

A.28. Exercise. Verify the following.
(1) Injective objects are T-acyclic for any left exact functor T.
(2) If f : X →Y is a continuous map then f∗is left exact. (Hint: use the adjunction
formula HomS(Y )(B, f∗(I)) ∼= HomS(X)(f ∗(B), I).)
(3) Fine, flabby, and soft sheaves are Γ-acyclic, f! acyclic and f∗acyclic. In particular,
for a complex of sheaves A• on a topological space, the derived functors H∗(A•) =
RΓ(A•), RHom(A•, B•), Rf∗(A•) and Rf!(A•) may be computed using any injective
or fine or flabby resolution of A•.
A.29. Examples.
1. The m-th derived functor of Hom is called Extm, (cf. Lemma A.15) i.e., it is the group
Extm(A•, B•) = Hm(RHom(A•, B•)) = Hm(Hom•(A•, J•)) = H0(Hom•(A•, J•[m]))
where B• →J• is an injective resolution. (We consider Hom(A•, B•) to be a functor of the B•
variable and derive it by injectively resolving. The same result can be obtained by projectively
resolving A•.) As in §1.8, there is a sheaf version of Hom, which also gives a sheaf version of Ext:
Extm(A•, B•)) = Hm(RHom(A•, B•))
2. The hypercohomology of RHom is exactly the group of homomorphisms in the derived category,
(A.29.1)
H0(X, RHom(A•, B•)) = HomDb(X)(A•, B•)
for complexes of sheaves A•, B• on a topological space X. (Take B• to be injective, then the group
H0(X, RHom(A•, B•)) is globally defined homotopy classes of morphisms A• →B• that commute
with the differentials.)
Exercise. Let G, H be abelian groups, as complexes in degree zero. Show that Ext1(G, H) coincides
with the usual definition of ExtZ(G, H),

A.30. The bad news. The derived category is not an abelian category. In fact, the homotopy
category of complexes is not an abelian category. Kernels and cokernels do not make sense in these
categories. The saving grace is that the cone operation still makes sense and in fact, it passes to
the homotopy category.

Appendix B. Transversality
Let K ⊂R be the Cantor set. Let f : R →R be a smooth (C∞) function that vanishes precisely
on K. Let A ⊂R2 denote the graph of f and let B denote the x-axis. Then A, B are smooth


PAGE 156
156
MARK GORESKY
submanifolds of R2 but their intersection is the Cantor set. This sort of unruly behavior can be
avoided using transversality.
Let A, B ⊂M be a smooth submanifolds of a smooth manifold M. Let {v1, · · · , vr} be vector
fields on M which span the tangent space at each point26 Let V be the r-dimensional vector space
of formal linear combinations of these vector fields. Let Φv denote the time = 1 flow of the vector
field v.
B.1. Theorem. The set of elements v ∈V such that Φv fails to take A transversally to B has
measure zero.
Proof. The method of proof (using Sard’s theorem, of course) is due originally to Marston Morse.
Let Φ : V × M →M be the time = 1 flow mapping. The assumptions on the vector fields imply
that Φ takes V × A transversally to B. (In fact for each a ∈A the partial map Φa : V →M
is a submersion.) So Φ−1(B) is a submanifold of V × M and it is transverse to V × A. Let
π : Φ−1(B) ∩(V × A) →V denote the projection. We claim the following are equivalent:
(1) v ∈V is a regular value of π
(2) {v} × A is transverse to Φ−1(B) in V × M
(3) Φv takes A transversally to B.
Equivalence of (2) and (3) is a short calculation. Equivalence of (1) and (2) is a dimension count.
First note that (V × A) is transverse to ϕ−1(B) in V × M, by the assumption on the vector
fields. Fix (w, a) ∈(V × A) ∩Φ−1(B). Let ˙A = TaA. Let
˙M = TaM, let ˙V = TwV and let
˙B = Tw,aΦ−1(B). So we have a diagram of vector spaces
˙V × ˙M
( ˙V × ˙A)
∪
6
∩˙B
Let r be the rank of the linear map π : ˙B ∩( ˙V × ˙A) →˙V ; its kernel is ˙B ∩({0} × A. Therefore
dim( ˙B ∩( ˙V × ˙A)) = r + dim( ˙B ∩({0} × ˙A)
or
b + (v + a) −m = r + (b + a −dim( ˙B + {0} × ˙A
where b = dim( ˙B), a = dim( ˙A), v = dim( ˙V ). Therefore
v −r = m −dim( ˙B + {0} × ˙A).
Therefore the map π is surjective (r = v) if and only if ˙B + {0} × ˙A spans ˙M.
Now, by Sard’s theorem the set of critical values v ∈V has measure zero. But this is exactly
the set of elements such that Φv fails to take A transversally to B.
□
26In fact we only need to assume that they span the normal space to B at each point in B.

LECTURE NOTES ON SHEAVES AND PERVERSE SHEAVES
157
B.2. Exactly the same argument shows, for example, that two submanifolds of Euclidean space
can be made transverse by an arbitrarily small translation. Similarly, two stratified subvarieties
of projective space can be made transverse by an arbitrarily small projective transformation. The
same method also applies to transversality of maps: if F : M ′ →M is a smooth mapping, if
A ⊂M ′ and if B ⊂M then by composing F with the time = 1 flow of an arbitrarily small smooth
vector field we can guarantee that the resulting map ˜F : M ′ →M takes A transversally to B.
